## Fueling and energy availability

<a id="fuel01"></a>
### FUEL01 — Systematic review: Carbohydrate supplementation on exercise performance or capacity of varying durations

**Stellingwerff T, Cox GR; 2014.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1139/apnm-2014-0027). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1139/apnm-2014-0027`. PMID: [24951297](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24951297/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review of randomised CHO vs placebo performance studies; Varied; relationship with exercise duration.
- **Population / baseline:** 679 subjects across 61 studies; not cycling-only.
- **Verified extraction:** 82% of studies showed a performance benefit vs placebo; percent gain correlated with exercise duration. Beyond about 2 h, high delivery (>90 g/h) and multiple-transportable CHO are discussed; near 1 h, oral exposure is the proposed mechanism.
- **Limitations:** Includes non-cycling endurance protocols; not a dose-response meta-analysis of 60 vs 90 vs 120 g/h.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1139/apnm-2014-0027)

<a id="fuel02"></a>
### FUEL02 — Carbohydrates for training and competition

**Burke LM, Hawley JA, Wong SH, Jeukendrup AE; 2011.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2011.585473). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1080/02640414.2011.585473`. PMID: [21660838](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21660838/).
- **Design / duration:** Consensus-style review; Event-scaled.
- **Population / baseline:** Endurance athletes; recommendations not a single trial.
- **Verified extraction:** Small amounts or mouth rinse for ~1 h; 30-60 g/h for longer sports; up to 90 g/h for events >2.5 h using blends. Whether extra train-low strategies improve performance in well-trained athletes was described as unclear.
- **Limitations:** Review, not a new trial. Daily g/kg bands were not in the retrieved abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2011.585473)

<a id="fuel03"></a>
### FUEL03 — A step towards personalized sports nutrition: carbohydrate intake during exercise

**Jeukendrup A; 2014.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-014-0148-z). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-014-0148-z`. PMID: [24791914](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24791914/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review, open full text used for transporter sentence; Scaled by exercise duration.
- **Population / baseline:** Endurance athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Single carbohydrate oxidised up to ~60 g/h (SGLT1 saturation stated in full text). ~90 g/h for ultra-endurance requires multiple transportable carbohydrates. Advice should be lowered when absolute intensity is low. Author also stated advice independent of body weight; later body-size data qualify that sentence.
- **Limitations:** Review. Body-weight independence is challenged by Ijaz 2025 for exogenous glucose oxidation.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/articles/PMC4008807), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-014-0148-z)

<a id="fuel04"></a>
### FUEL04 — Curvilinear dose-response relationship of carbohydrate (0-120 g/h) and performance

**Smith JW, Pascoe DD, Passe DH, Ruby BC, Stewart LK, Baker LB, Zachwieja JJ; 2013.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e31827205d1). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/mss.0b013e31827205d1`. PMID: [22968309](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22968309/).
- **Design / duration:** Multi-site double-blind crossover; 2 h constant load plus 20 km time trial.
- **Population / baseline:** 51 cyclists and triathletes, 28 ± 7 years.
- **Verified extraction:** Estimated incremental gains of 1.0% to 4.7% from 9 to 78 g/h, with diminishing returns above 78 g/h. Best mean performance near 78 g/h of 1:1:1 glucose-fructose-maltodextrin.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Not characterised as elite. One fluid rate (1 L/h).
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e31827205d1)

<a id="fuel05"></a>
### FUEL05 — A narrative review of the high-carbohydrate fueling revolution (≥100 g/h) in the professional peloton

**Wilson PB; 2026.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-025-02372-6). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-025-02372-6`. PMID: [41343040](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41343040/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; open full text read for key points and ACSM quotation; Single-session and multi-day racing discussed separately.
- **Population / baseline:** Professional cycling context; synthesises laboratory trials.
- **Verified extraction:** Experiments comparing ≥100 g/h with 60-90 g/h do not clearly show less endogenous carbohydrate use or better performance. Studies do not mimic multi-day stage racing. Possible risks include gut symptoms and, if over-applied in training, a smaller metabolic signal. Quotes 2016 ACSM statement that events ≥2.5 h are associated with better performance up to 90 g/h.
- **Limitations:** Narrative review. Multi-day benefit is theoretical/observational, not an RCT.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12982284/), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-025-02372-6)

<a id="fuel07"></a>
### FUEL07 — Carbohydrate dose influences liver and muscle glycogen oxidation and performance during prolonged exercise

**King AJ, O'Hara JP, Morrison DJ, Preston T, King RFGJ; 2018.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.14814/phy2.13555). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.14814/phy2.13555`. PMID: [29333721](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29333721/).
- **Design / duration:** Double-blind randomised crossover; 2 h at 77% VO2max plus 30 min time trial.
- **Population / baseline:** 10 trained males.
- **Verified extraction:** No linear dose-response. 90 g/h glucose-fructose was the most favourable time-trial condition versus 60, 75, and 112.5 g/h and placebo in the abstract conclusion.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only for numbers used here. Magnitude-based language in the abstract. Small n.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.14814/phy2.13555)

<a id="fuel08"></a>
### FUEL08 — New horizons in carbohydrate research and application for endurance athletes

**Podlogar T, Wallis GA; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01757-1). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-022-01757-1`. PMID: [36173597](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36173597/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; open full text used for SGLT1/GLUT5 and ratio paragraph; Review.
- **Population / baseline:** Endurance athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Names SGLT1 and GLUT5. Recommends glucose-fructose mixtures beyond ~2.5 h at 60-90 g/h. Argues a ratio near 1:0.8 is superior for oxidation efficiency and gut comfort. Contemporary view: train-low has limited utility for performance benefits.
- **Limitations:** Review interpretation of ratio superiority goes beyond the small primary trials.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/articles/PMC9734239)

<a id="fuel09"></a>
### FUEL09 — Increased exogenous but unaltered endogenous carbohydrate oxidation with fructose-maltodextrin at 120 vs 90 g/h

**Podlogar T, Bokal Š, Cirnski S, Wallis GA; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-022-05019-w). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-022-05019-w`. PMID: [35951130](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35951130/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover, stable-isotope oxidation; 3 h at 95% gas-exchange threshold, overnight fasted.
- **Population / baseline:** 11 highly trained men, VO2peak 62.6 ± 7 mL/kg/min.
- **Verified extraction:** Exogenous oxidation 1.51 ± 0.22 vs 1.29 ± 0.16 g/min (120 vs 90 g/h). Endogenous oxidation not different (2.15 ± 0.30 vs 2.20 ± 0.33 g/min). No performance test. Ratios also differed (0.8:1 vs 1:2).
- **Limitations:** Low absolute intensity. Ratio and dose confounded. Not a performance trial.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-022-05019-w)

**Wallis GA, Rowlands DS, Shaw C, Jentjens RL, Jeukendrup AE; 2005.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/01.mss.0000155399.23358.82). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/01.mss.0000155399.23358.82`. PMID: [15741841](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15741841/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised crossover; 150 min at 55% Wmax.
- **Population / baseline:** 8 trained cyclists, VO2max 64.1 ± 3.1.
- **Verified extraction:** Peak exogenous oxidation ~1.50 vs 1.06 g/min for maltodextrin+fructose vs maltodextrin at 1.8 g/min.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Small male sample. Oxidation, not a race.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1249/01.mss.0000155399.23358.82)

<a id="fuel11"></a>
### FUEL11 — Superior endurance performance with ingestion of multiple transportable carbohydrates

**Currell K, Jeukendrup AE; 2008.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e31815adf19). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/mss.0b013e31815adf19`. PMID: [18202575](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18202575/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover performance trial; 120 min at 55% Wmax plus ~1 h time trial.
- **Population / baseline:** 8 trained male cyclists, VO2max 64.7 ± 3.9, Wmax 364 ± 31 W.
- **Verified extraction:** Abstract conclusion: 2:1 glucose:fructose at 1.8 g/min was 8% faster than isocaloric glucose and 19% faster than water. Reported completion times in the abstract conflict with that direction and were not used.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. n=8. Time figures internally inconsistent.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)

**O'Brien WJ, Rowlands DS; 2011.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1152/ajpgi.00419.2010). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1152/ajpgi.00419.2010`. PMID: [21071509](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21071509/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised double-blind crossover; 150 min at 50% peak power then incremental test.
- **Population / baseline:** 10 cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** At 1.8 g/min, 0.8 fructose:maltodextrin had small oxidation advantages vs 0.5 and 1.25 and the lowest gut-symptom increases. Moderate peak-power improvements for 0.8 and 1.25 vs 0.5.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Small sample. Magnitude-based inference.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1152/ajpgi.00419.2010)

**O'Brien WJ, Stannard SR, Clarke JA, Rowlands DS; 2013.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e31828e12d4). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/mss.0b013e31828e12d4`. PMID: [23949097](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23949097/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover; 2 h at 57% peak power then 10 sprints.
- **Population / baseline:** 12 male cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** 0.8 ratio had higher exogenous CHO oxidation efficiency and moderately higher sprint power than 0.5 and 1.25. Fluid-absorption effects inconclusive.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Sprint outcome, not a long time trial.

<a id="fuel14"></a>
### FUEL14 — Fructose-glucose composite carbohydrates and endurance performance: critical review

**Rowlands DS, Houltham S, Musa-Veloso K, Brown F, Paulionis L, Bailey D; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-015-0381-0). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-015-0381-0`. PMID: [26373645](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26373645/).
- **Design / duration:** Critical review of performance studies; 2.5-3.0 h estimates.
- **Population / baseline:** 14 studies, mostly male cycling, ~2.5-3 h.
- **Verified extraction:** 0.5-1.0:1 fructose:glucose/maltodextrin at 1.3-2.4 g/min associated with 1-9% mean-power gains vs isocaloric single saccharide. Larger effects at higher ingestion rates. 0.8:1 highlighted at 1.5-1.8 g/min.
- **Limitations:** Mostly men. Not a formal meta-analysis with a single pooled estimate in the abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-015-0381-0)

### FUEL15 — Multiple transportable carbohydrates enhance gastric emptying and fluid delivery

**Jeukendrup AE, Moseley L; 2010.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0838.2008.00862.x). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/j.1600-0838.2008.00862.x`. PMID: [19000102](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19000102/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover; 120 min cycling at 61% VO2max.
- **Population / baseline:** 8 men, VO2max 62.6 ± 2.5.
- **Verified extraction:** Glucose+fructose (2:1, 1.5 g/min) emptied faster and delivered fluid faster than glucose alone, and attenuated heart-rate rise.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Delivery, not performance. Small n.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)

<a id="fuel16"></a>
### FUEL16 — Fructose and sucrose intake increase exogenous carbohydrate oxidation during exercise

**Trommelen J, Fuchs CJ, Beelen M, Lenaerts K, Jeukendrup AE, Cermak NM, van Loon LJ; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.3390/nu9020167). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.3390/nu9020167`. PMID: [28230742](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28230742/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover, stable isotope; 180 min at 50% Wmax.
- **Population / baseline:** 10 trained male cyclists, VO2peak 65 ± 2.
- **Verified extraction:** Ten trained men, 180 min at 50% Wmax, 1.8 g/min (108 g/h). Peak exogenous oxidation 0.96 ± 0.06 g/min glucose alone; 1.40 ± 0.06 for 1.2 g/min glucose + 0.6 g/min fructose; 1.29 ± 0.07 for 0.6 g/min glucose + 1.2 g/min sucrose. The sucrose arm hydrolyses to the same overall 2:1 glucose:fructose as the free mix. Sucrose alone was not tested. Both mixed arms about 46% above glucose alone.
- **Limitations:** Not a sucrose-alone versus free glucose-plus-fructose comparison. Moderate intensity. Men only. Open access PMC5331598.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)

- **Design / duration:** Randomised crossover; 150 min at 50% Wmax.

### FUEL20 — Ingestion of glucose or sucrose prevents liver but not muscle glycogen depletion during prolonged exercise in trained cyclists

**Gonzalez JT, Fuchs CJ, Smith FE, et al.; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1152/ajpendo.00376.2015). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1152/ajpendo.00376.2015`. PMID: [26487008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26487008/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover with 13C-MRS; 3 h at 50% peak power; 1.7 g/min (102 g/h).
- **Population / baseline:** 14 cyclists; 4 also did water.
- **Verified extraction:** Liver glycogen did not fall with glucose or sucrose; muscle glycogen fell similarly. Whole-body CHO use higher with sucrose (2.03 ± 0.43 vs 1.66 ± 0.36 g/min). Water reference: both liver and muscle glycogen fell.

**Fuchs CJ, Gonzalez JT, Beelen M, et al.; 2016.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1152/japplphysiol.01023.2015). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1152/japplphysiol.01023.2015`. PMID: [27013608](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27013608/).

<a id="fuel24"></a>
### FUEL24 — Effect of gut-training and feeding-challenge on gastrointestinal status: systematic review

**Martinez IG, Mika AS, Biesiekierski JR, Costa RJS; 2023.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-023-01841-0). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-023-01841-0`. PMID: [37061651](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37061651/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review, 8 studies; 4-28 days.
- **Population / baseline:** Endurance exercise gut-training trials.
- **Verified extraction:** Some 2-week CHO protocols reduced discomfort (~47% in two studies) and malabsorption (45-54% in two; no change in one). Several symptom results unclear. Gastric emptying and injury markers generally unchanged.
- **Limitations:** Heterogeneous tools. Few studies. Mostly not cycling performance.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-023-01841-0)

<a id="fuel25"></a>
### FUEL25 — Short-term very high carbohydrate diet and gut-training have minor effects in highly trained endurance athletes

**King AJ, Etxebarria N, Ross ML, et al.; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.3390/nu14091929). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.3390/nu14091929`. PMID: [35565896](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35565896/).
- **Design / duration:** Two cohorts, intervention vs guideline-range CHO; 2 weeks diet plus gut training; 26 km race-walk or 35 km run.
- **Population / baseline:** 19 elite male race walkers; 18 highly trained distance runners.
- **Verified extraction:** Large individual variability. No clear performance advantage of pushing above guideline CHO. Some increase in upper-GI symptoms. Authors note confounding weight loss and fitness gains.
- **Limitations:** Not a cycling RCT. Abstract-only for numbers used.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.3390/nu14091929)

<a id="fuel26"></a>
### FUEL26 — Daily training with high carbohydrate availability increases exogenous carbohydrate oxidation

**Cox GR, Clark SA, Cox AJ, et al.; 2010.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1152/japplphysiol.00950.2009). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1152/japplphysiol.00950.2009`. PMID: [20466803](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20466803/).
- **Design / duration:** Pair-matched 28-day training intervention; 28 days; tests were 100 min steady state plus 7 kJ/kg time trial.
- **Population / baseline:** 16 endurance-trained cyclists or triathletes.
- **Verified extraction:** High group increased citrate synthase and exogenous glucose oxidation. Performance improved ~6% in training; metabolic differences did not change the magnitude of the performance gain vs energy-matched low group.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Small groups (n=8).
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1152/japplphysiol.00950.2009)

### FUEL27 — No performance effects of altered carbohydrate distribution during intense cycling

**Podlogar T, Grmek B, Pungerčar L, Cirnski S, Goršek Šparovec T, Rowlands DS; 2026.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijsnem.2025-0059). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1123/ijsnem.2025-0059`. PMID: [41468185](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41468185/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised crossover; 180 min intermittent preload, 15 min all-out, sprint to exhaustion; 90 g/h.
- **Population / baseline:** 12 trained male cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** Increasing, decreasing, and constant patterns did not clearly differ for 15-min performance or time to exhaustion (wide confidence limits). Decreasing pattern had higher CHO oxidation than constant and less late nausea than increasing.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Same total dose; not a dose trial.

### FUEL28 — Training with low muscle glycogen enhances fat metabolism in well-trained cyclists

**Hulston CJ, Venables MC, Mann CH, et al.; 2010.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e3181dd5070). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/mss.0b013e3181dd5070`. PMID: [20351596](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20351596/).
- **Design / duration:** Pair-matched 3-week training; 3 weeks; 9 aerobic and 9 HIT sessions.
- **Population / baseline:** 14 well-trained cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** Low-glycogen group interval power 297 ± 8 W vs 323 ± 9 W in high. Fat oxidation increased. Performance no more effective than high-glycogen training. P value for power contrast was truncated in the retrieved abstract.

<a id="fuel36"></a>
### FUEL36 — Performance effects of periodized carbohydrate restriction in endurance trained athletes: systematic review and meta-analysis

**Gejl KD, Nybo L; 2021.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-021-00435-3). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1186/s12970-021-00435-3`. PMID: [34001184](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34001184/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; ≥1 week, restriction ≥3 times/week.
- **Population / baseline:** 9 studies; VO2max ≥55 (women) or ≥60 (men) mL/kg/min.
- **Verified extraction:** SMD 0.17 (95% CI -0.15 to 0.49), P=0.29 versus high carbohydrate availability. No demonstrated reliable average performance advantage. The interval permits a potentially meaningful positive effect, so this is not evidence that the true effect is zero.
- **Limitations:** Nonsignificant pooled effect is imprecise, not proof of no benefit. Includes the positive Marquet trials. Heterogeneous protocols.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-021-00435-3)

<a id="fuel53"></a>
### FUEL53 — ISSN position stand: nutrient timing

**Kerksick CM, Arent S, Schoenfeld BJ, et al.; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-017-0189-4). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1186/s12970-017-0189-4`. PMID: [28919842](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28919842/).
- **Design / duration:** Position stand; Daily and peri-exercise.
- **Population / baseline:** Healthy exercising adults.
- **Verified extraction:** Intact sentence used: glycogen stores maximised by 8-12 g carbohydrate/kg/day. Protein doses 0.25-0.40 g/kg or 20-40 g every 3-4 h. During-exercise 30-60 g/h sentence exists but is a conservative general range, not the best cyclist source.
- **Limitations:** Parts of the Europe PMC abstract are corrupted. Only intact sentences used. Broader than trained cyclists.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-017-0189-4)

<a id="fuel54"></a>
### FUEL54 — ISSN position stand: protein and exercise

**Jäger R, Kerksick CM, Campbell BI, et al.; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-017-0177-8). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1186/s12970-017-0177-8`. PMID: [28642676](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28642676/).
- **Design / duration:** Position stand; Daily.
- **Population / baseline:** Healthy exercising individuals.
- **Verified extraction:** 1.4-2.0 g protein/kg/day for most exercising people; 0.25 g/kg or 20-40 g per serving. Endurance athletes should prioritise carbohydrate; protein may help offset damage.
- **Limitations:** Not an endurance dose-finding trial.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Fueling and energy availability](../topics/fueling-energy-availability.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12970-017-0177-8)

- **Population / baseline:** 6 male endurance-trained adults, VO2peak 60.3 ± 6.7, after a 20 km run.

**Sawka MN, Burke LM, Eichner ER, Maughan RJ, Montain SJ, Stachenfeld NS; 2007.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0b013e31802ca597). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

- **Design / duration:** Position stand; Before, during, after exercise.
- **Population / baseline:** Physically active individuals.
- **Verified extraction:** Start euhydrated. Prevent excessive dehydration defined as >2% body-weight loss from water deficit. Customise fluid replacement. Estimate sweat rate from body mass.

### FUEL72 — Menstrual disruption with exercise is not linked to an energy availability threshold

**Lieberman JL, De Souza MJ, Wagstaff DA, Williams NI; 2018.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/mss.0000000000001451). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/mss.0000000000001451`. PMID: [29023359](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29023359/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised diet-and-exercise intervention over three menstrual cycles; secondary EA analysis; Three menstrual cycles.
- **Population / baseline:** 35 sedentary ovulatory women, 18-24 years.
- **Verified extraction:** Odds of menstrual disturbance fell 9% per unit rise in EA (OR 0.91, 95% CI 0.84-0.98). No threshold. Tertiles ~23-50 kcal/kg FFM/day.

**Melin AK, Heikura IA, Tenforde A, Mountjoy M; 2019.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijsnem.2018-0201). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

**Stellingwerff T, Heikura IA, Meeusen R, et al.; 2021.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-021-01491-0). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-021-01491-0`. PMID: [34181189](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34181189/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review of overload studies that also measured intake; Review.
- **Population / baseline:** 21 overload/OTS-type studies with intake data.
- **Verified extraction:** 18 of 21 studies showed an EA decrease/difference or a CHO availability decrease during overload. Symptom overlap with RED-S is large. Under-fueling should be excluded before an overtraining diagnosis.

**Ijaz A, Collins AJ, Moreno-Cabañas A, et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijsnem.2024-0097). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1123/ijsnem.2024-0097`. PMID: [39332815](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39332815/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover/group comparison with 13C glucose; 120 min at 95% lactate threshold; 90 g/h glucose.
- **Population / baseline:** 15 cyclists grouped by body mass; lactate threshold matched at 2.3 W/kg.
- **Verified extraction:** Exogenous glucose oxidation 33 ± 8 g/h in smaller vs 45 ± 13 g/h in larger athletes at the same relative intensity (difference 13 g/h, 95% CI 2-24). Body surface area vs peak exogenous oxidation r=0.85.

## Sleep, heat and iron

<a id="rec01"></a>
### REC01 — Sleep and the athlete: narrative review and 2021 expert consensus recommendations

**Walsh NP et al.; 2021.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2020-102025). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1136/bjsports-2020-102025`. PMID: [33144349](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33144349/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review and expert consensus; n/a.
- **Population / baseline:** Elite athletes; consensus panel.
- **Verified extraction:** Habitual short sleep (<7 h) and fragmentation are common. Total sleep loss reduces performance; partial restriction over 1-3 nights remains unclear. Rejects a one-size 7-9 h rule; calls for research on naps and sleep extension.
- **Limitations:** Few female experimental data; not a meta-analysis of cycling.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:33144349&format=json&resultType=core)

### REC02 — Effects of Acute Sleep Loss on Physical Performance: A Systematic and Meta-Analytical Review

**Craven J et al.; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y`. PMID: [35708888](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35708888/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; Acute (≤6 h vs >6 h in 24 h).
- **Population / baseline:** 69 publications; 89% male outcome measures.
- **Verified extraction:** Mean performance change -7.56% (95% CI -11.9 to -3.13), including endurance. Consistent harm with deprivation and late restriction, not all early-night restriction. Afternoon tasks more affected. ~0.4% worse per hour awake in deprivation/late-restriction protocols.
- **Limitations:** I2=98.1%. Not cyclist-specific.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:35708888&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://europepmc.org/articles/PMC9584849)

<a id="rec03"></a>
### REC03 — Effects of sleep deprivation on sports performance and perceived exertion in athletes and non-athletes

**Kong Y et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.3389/fphys.2025.1544286). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.3389/fphys.2025.1544286`. PMID: [40236824](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40236824/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; Acute deprivation/restriction; search to Sep 2024.
- **Population / baseline:** 45 studies; athletes and non-athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Athlete aerobic endurance SMD -0.66 (95% CI -1.28 to -0.04); RPE SMD 0.39 (0.11 to 0.66). Total deprivation linked to aerobic endurance. PM tests more affected for several outcomes.
- **Limitations:** Wide CIs; mixed sports.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:40236824&format=json&resultType=core)

<a id="rec04"></a>
### REC04 — Effects of Acute Sleep Deprivation on Sporting Performance in Athletes

**Gong M et al.; 2024.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.2147/NSS.S467531). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.2147/NSS.S467531`. PMID: [39006249](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39006249/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; Acute sleep deprivation.
- **Population / baseline:** 27 studies, athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Overall athletic performance impaired (effect size -0.56 in the retrieved abstract fragment). Aerobic endurance among impaired abilities. Larger impact from end-of-night partial loss than beginning-of-night loss. Afternoon worse than morning.
- **Limitations:** Abstract numeric formatting was partly corrupted by markup; directional findings used, not every SMD.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:39006249&format=json&resultType=core)

<a id="rec05"></a>
### REC05 — Extended Sleep Maintains Endurance Performance Better than Normal or Restricted Sleep

**Roberts SSH et al.; 2019.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000002071). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/MSS.0000000000002071`. PMID: [31246714](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31246714/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised crossover; 3 nights restriction, normal, or extension; TT on 4 days.
- **Population / baseline:** 9 endurance cyclists/triathletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Restriction ~4.5-4.9 h slowed day-3 TT (60.4±3.7 vs 58.8±2.5 min). Extension ~7.9-8.6 h vs normal ~6.5-7.1 h made day-4 TT faster (56.8±3.1 vs 58.7±3.4 min). Authors conclude sleep >8 h to optimise performance.
- **Limitations:** n=9. Acute repeated TT, not training adaptation. Abstract-only.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:31246714&format=json&resultType=core)

<a id="rec06"></a>
### REC06 — Effects of total sleep deprivation on endurance cycling performance and heart rate indices

**Roberts SSH et al.; 2019.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2019.1661561). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1080/02640414.2019.1661561`. PMID: [31526108](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31526108/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover; 1 night total deprivation vs normal sleep.
- **Population / baseline:** 13 endurance athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** TT 10% slower after deprivation (64±7 vs 59±4 min) and 11% slower than prior day. RPE:HR ratio, mood, and vigilance worsened.
- **Limitations:** Total deprivation, not partial. Abstract-only.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:31526108&format=json&resultType=core)

<a id="rec07"></a>
### REC07 — The effects of sleep extension on the athletic performance of collegiate basketball players

**Mah CD et al.; 2011.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.5665/SLEEP.1132). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.5665/SLEEP.1132`. PMID: [21731144](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21731144/).
- **Design / duration:** Pre-post sleep extension, no non-extension control; 5-7 weeks, goal ≥10 h in bed.
- **Population / baseline:** 11 male collegiate basketball players.
- **Verified extraction:** Objective sleep +110.9±79.7 min. Sprint 16.2±0.61 to 15.5±0.54 s. Free-throw and 3-point accuracy about +9 percentage points.
- **Limitations:** Not cycling or endurance. No control group.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec10"></a>
### REC10 — The impact of training schedules on the sleep and fatigue of elite athletes

**Sargent C et al.; 2014.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.3109/07420528.2014.957306). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.3109/07420528.2014.957306`. PMID: [25222347](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25222347/).
- **Design / duration:** Observational actigraphy; 2 weeks normal training.
- **Population / baseline:** 70 nationally ranked athletes, 7 sports.
- **Verified extraction:** Mean sleep 6 h 30 min. Less time in bed and less sleep on nights before training days than rest days. Shorter sleep associated with higher pre-training fatigue.
- **Limitations:** Not an experimental need study. Mixed sports.

<a id="rec11"></a>
### REC11 — How Much Sleep Does an Elite Athlete Need?

**Sargent C et al.; 2021.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijspp.2020-0896). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1123/ijspp.2020-0896`. PMID: [34021090](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34021090/).
- **Design / duration:** Observational actigraphy plus self-assessed need; ~12 nights.
- **Population / baseline:** 175 elite athletes (30 women), 12 sports.
- **Verified extraction:** Self-assessed need 8.3±0.9 h; obtained 6.7±0.8 h; deficit 96±60.6 min. 3% met own need. Individual-sport athletes slept less; stated need similar.
- **Limitations:** Self-assessed need is not an experimental optimum and is not 9 h.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:34021090&format=json&resultType=core)

<a id="rec14"></a>
### REC14 — Is daytime napping an effective strategy to improve sport-related cognitive and physical performance and reduce fatigue?

**Mesas AE et al.; 2023.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2022-106355). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1136/bjsports-2022-106355`. PMID: [36690376](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36690376/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis of RCTs; Daytime naps vs no nap.
- **Population / baseline:** 22 trials, 291 men aged 18-35.
- **Verified extraction:** After normal sleep, physical performance SMD 0.99 (0.67 to 1.31), I2=89%. Benefits higher for 30 to <60 min and >1 h from waking to test. Also favourable after partial deprivation on fewer studies.
- **Limitations:** No women. High heterogeneity. Not cycling TT.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:36690376&format=json&resultType=core)

### REC28 — The Effects of Heat Adaptation on Physiology, Perception and Exercise Performance in the Heat: A Meta-Analysis

**Tyler CJ et al.; 2016.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-016-0538-5). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-016-0538-5`. PMID: [27106556](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27106556/).
- **Design / duration:** Meta-analysis; Most often 7-14 days; longer >14 days more effective.
- **Population / baseline:** 96 articles; human heat-adaptation studies.
- **Verified extraction:** Moderate benefit on exercise capacity and performance in the heat. Longer regimens more effective. Lowers core temperature and supports cardiovascular stability and heat loss.
- **Limitations:** Performance synthesis is in the heat, not cool cycling. Large methodological heterogeneity.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:27106556&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-016-0538-5)

<a id="rec29"></a>
### REC29 — Influence of Exercise Heat Acclimation Protocol Characteristics on Adaptation Kinetics

**McDonald P et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1002/cph4.70017). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1002/cph4.70017`. PMID: [40442924](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40442924/).
- **Design / duration:** Bayesian meta-regression; Mean 8±4 exposures, 90±36 min, 39.1±4.8°C.
- **Population / baseline:** 211 papers.
- **Verified extraction:** Plasma volume +5.6% (90% CrI 3.8 to 7.0). Time-to-exhaustion +49% (35 to 61). Time-trial +3.1% (1.8 to 4.5). Extra exposure associated with +1.9 g haemoglobin mass (0.6 to 3.2), an across-study slope.
- **Limitations:** Heat-performance outcomes. Hb-mass slope is not a daily prescription.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:40442924&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1002/cph4.70017)

<a id="rec30"></a>
### REC30 — The effect of post-exercise heat exposure (passive heat acclimation) on endurance exercise performance

**Solomon TPJ, Laye MJ; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-024-01038-6). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1186/s13102-024-01038-6`. PMID: [39762944](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39762944/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; Post-exercise sauna or hot-water immersion vs same training.
- **Population / baseline:** 10 studies, 199 adults (156 male).
- **Verified extraction:** Hot performance ratio of means 1.04 (95% CI 0.94-1.15). Thermoneutral performance trivial. Certainty low to very low.
- **Limitations:** Small samples, bias, imprecision. Does not erase single positive trials.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:39762944&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1186/s13102-024-01038-6)

<a id="rec31"></a>
### REC31 — Effects of Post-Exercise Heat Exposure on Acute Recovery and Training-Induced Performance Adaptations

**Ahokas EK et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-025-00910-0). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1186/s40798-025-00910-0`. PMID: [41032138](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41032138/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review; Acute and chronic post-exercise heat ≥36°C.
- **Population / baseline:** 14 studies, 194 adults.
- **Verified extraction:** Acute recovery mixed. Chronic studies suggest possible running benefit in heat; no clear cycling performance or VO2max effect in included studies. Evidence low to moderate.
- **Limitations:** Heterogeneity prevented meta-analysis.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:41032138&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1186/s40798-025-00910-0)

<a id="rec32"></a>
### REC32 — Adaptations and mechanisms of human heat acclimation

**Périard JD, Racinais S, Sawka MN; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12408). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/sms.12408`. PMID: [25943654](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25943654/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; n/a.
- **Population / baseline:** Athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Heat acclimation improves sweating, skin blood flow, lowers body temperature, reduces cardiovascular strain, and can improve hot and potentially temperate aerobic performance. Dose depends on intensity, duration, frequency, and number of exposures.
- **Limitations:** Review, not a new trial. Temperate benefit described as potential.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:25943654&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12408)

<a id="rec33"></a>
### REC33 — Consensus recommendations on training and competing in the heat

**Racinais S et al.; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2015-094915). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1136/bjsports-2015-094915`. PMID: [26069301](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26069301/).
- **Design / duration:** Consensus; 1-2 weeks repeated exercise-heat.
- **Population / baseline:** Athletes competing in heat.
- **Verified extraction:** Heat acclimatisation is the main intervention to reduce strain and protect performance. Start euhydrated and minimise dehydration.
- **Limitations:** Consensus, not a cyclist RCT.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:26069301&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2015-094915)

<a id="rec34"></a>
### REC34 — Heat Acclimation Decay and Re-Induction: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis

**Daanen HAM, Racinais S, Périard JD; 2018.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-017-0808-x). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-017-0808-x`. PMID: [29129022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29129022/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; HA ≥5 days; decay ≥7 days in longitudinal studies.
- **Population / baseline:** 12 studies for decay.
- **Verified extraction:** End-exercise HR adaptation lost ~2.3%/decay day; core temperature ~2.6%/day. Re-induction faster than original induction for HR and core temperature.
- **Limitations:** Protocols poorly standardised. Not haemoglobin mass.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:29129022&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-017-0808-x)

<a id="rec35"></a>
### REC35 — Effect of post-exercise sauna bathing on the endurance performance of competitive male runners

**Scoon GS et al.; 2007.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2006.06.009). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1016/j.jsams.2006.06.009`. PMID: [16877041](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16877041/).
- **Design / duration:** Crossover; 3 weeks sauna vs 3 weeks control; 31±5 min at 89.9±2.0°C; 12.7±2.1 sessions.
- **Population / baseline:** 6 male distance runners.
- **Verified extraction:** TTE +32% (90% CL 21-43%), equated to ~1.9% TT. Plasma volume +7.1% (5.6-8.7). Red-cell volume +3.5% (-0.8 to 8.1). Performance correlated with plasma and blood volume, not clearly red-cell volume.
- **Limitations:** n=6. TTE. Not 3x45. Not cyclists.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:16877041&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2006.06.009)

<a id="rec36"></a>
### REC36 — Effect of sauna-based heat acclimation on plasma volume and heart rate variability

**Stanley J et al.; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-014-3060-1). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-014-3060-1`. PMID: [25432420](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25432420/).
- **Design / duration:** Time series within athletes; 10 days, 30 min, 87°C, 11% RH, post training.
- **Population / baseline:** 7 well-trained male cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** Plasma volume +17.8% (90% CL 7.4-29.2) after four exposures. HR/HRV tracking of plasma volume unclear. No performance test.
- **Limitations:** n=7. Magnitude-based inference. Capillary plasma-volume method.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:25432420&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-014-3060-1)

### REC37 — Post-exercise hot water immersion induces heat acclimation and improves endurance exercise performance in the heat

**Zurawlew MJ et al.; 2016.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12638). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/sms.12638`. PMID: [26661992](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26661992/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled intervention; 6 days; 40 min run then ≤40 min 40°C (n=10) or 34°C (n=7).
- **Population / baseline:** 17 non-heat-acclimatised men.

- **Limitations:** Not trained cyclists.

- **Design / duration:** Controlled comparison; 6 days post-exercise 40°C immersion.
- **Population / baseline:** 8 endurance-trained (~9 h/week) and 8 recreational (~3 h/week) men.

**Kirby NV et al.; 2021.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-020-04541-z). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

### REC50 — Haematological adaptations to high-altitude and heat acclimation training in elite male cyclists

**Cubel C et al.; 2026.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1113/EP092968). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1113/EP092968`. PMID: [40929090](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40929090/).
- **Design / duration:** Within-athlete comparison of camps; 21 days at 3000 m; heat was six 1 h sessions/week for 5 weeks.

- **Limitations:** Small paired n. Not a shared-mechanism proof. First published 2025-09-10.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:40929090&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1113/EP092968)

<a id="rec51"></a>
### REC51 — Heat Suit Training Preserves the Increased Hemoglobin Mass after Altitude Camp in Elite Cyclists

**Rønnestad BR et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003542). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/MSS.0000000000003542`. PMID: [39160765](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39160765/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled maintenance after altitude; 3 heat-suit sessions/week for 3.5 weeks after 3 weeks at ~2100 m; duration not in abstract.
- **Population / baseline:** 18 elite male cyclists, VO2max 76±5.
- **Verified extraction:** Camp +4.1% Hb mass. Heat maintained (0.2%) vs control -3.3%. Heat plasma volume +11.6%.
- **Limitations:** Maintenance, not sea-level induction. Session length not in retrieved abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:39160765&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003542)

<a id="rec52"></a>
### REC52 — Long-term passive heat acclimation enhances maximal oxygen consumption via haematological and cardiac adaptation in endurance runners

**Jenkins EJ et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP289874). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1113/JP289874`. PMID: [41267396](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41267396/).
- **Design / duration:** Within-subject counterbalanced crossover; ≥40°C hot-water immersion, 45 min, 5x/week, 5 weeks.
- **Population / baseline:** 10 well-trained runners (9 male), VO2max 64.5±8.1.
- **Verified extraction:** Hb mass +33 g (95% CI 18 to 49). Blood volume +284 ml. VO2max +2.7 ml/kg/min (1.4 to 4.1).
- **Limitations:** Not 3x/week. Not cyclists. Not sauna. Small n. First published 2025-11-20.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:41267396&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP289874)

<a id="rec53"></a>
### REC53 — Mechanisms of haemoglobin mass expansion following heat stress

**Jenkins EJ et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP288997). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1113/JP288997`. PMID: [40836483](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40836483/).
- **Design / duration:** Mechanistic review; n/a.
- **Population / baseline:** n/a.
- **Verified extraction:** Argues long-term heat acclimation (≥5 weeks) can expand Hb mass ~2-4% via erythropoiesis that diverges from hypoxia pathways (renal oxygen tension, plasma-volume signals, cellular stress). Passive heat is discussed as unproven at the time of that review relative to exercise-heat.
- **Limitations:** Review claim that every study showed concurrent VO2max gains is stronger than several original trials retrieved here.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:40836483&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP288997)

<a id="rec54"></a>
### REC54 — Altitude or heat training to increase haemoglobin mass and endurance exercise performance in elite sport

**Lundby C, Robach P; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1113/JP287700). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1113/JP287700`. PMID: [41139221](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41139221/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; 3-5 weeks heat training.
- **Population / baseline:** Elite endurance sport.
- **Verified extraction:** Heat training repeatedly raises Hb mass; associated performance gains are only small. Should not displace the rest of elite preparation. Individual choice versus altitude.
- **Limitations:** Review, not a new trial.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec56"></a>
### REC56 — Hematological Adaptations to Post-Exercise Sauna Bathing With No Fluid Intake

**Sitkowski D et al.; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1080/02701367.2021.1921684). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1080/02701367.2021.1921684`. PMID: [34727008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34727008/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised crossover; 3x/week, 4 weeks, 30 min sauna at 89±3°C after 60 min cycling; no fluid.
- **Population / baseline:** 13 physical-education students.
- **Verified extraction:** No additional Hb mass, EPO, or fitness benefit versus training alone. Hb-mass changes within typical error <1.8%.
- **Limitations:** Students. Fluid withheld. 30 not 45 min.

<a id="rec57"></a>
### REC57 — Limited benefit of repeated post-exercise hot water immersion on exercise performance of well-trained cyclists

**Kjertakov M, Aughey R, Petersen A; 2026.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-025-06041-4). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-025-06041-4`. PMID: [41432746](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41432746/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled intervention; 6 days; 40 min at 50% PPO in ~14°C then 40 min hot or thermoneutral water.
- **Population / baseline:** 16 well-trained male cyclists.
- **Verified extraction:** No 20 km TT benefit at 27°C or 35°C. Some perceptual and HR changes at 27°C. First published 2025-12-23.
- **Limitations:** Short passive protocol. n=8 per arm.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec58"></a>
### REC58 — A comparison of medium-term heat acclimation by post-exercise hot water immersion or exercise in the heat

**McIntyre RD et al.; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1152/ajpregu.00315.2021). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1152/ajpregu.00315.2021`. PMID: [36094449](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36094449/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised comparison; Up to 12 days post-exercise 40°C bath vs exercise at 33°C or 19°C.
- **Population / baseline:** Men; recreational model.
- **Verified extraction:** 12 days immersion gave fuller thermal adaptation than exercise-heat without differences in endurance capacity or overreaching markers including sleep (P≥0.190).
- **Limitations:** Not elite cyclists.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec59"></a>
### REC59 — Iron considerations for the athlete: a narrative review

**Sim M et al.; 2019.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-019-04157-y). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-019-04157-y`. PMID: [31055680](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31055680/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; n/a.
- **Population / baseline:** Athlete cohorts.
- **Verified extraction:** Iron deficiency about 15-35% of female athlete cohorts and 5-11% of male cohorts in summarised studies. Hepcidin highlighted as a regulator of athlete iron metabolism.
- **Limitations:** Prevalence depends on cut-off and cohort.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec60"></a>
### REC60 — Iron deficiency in sports - definition, influence on performance and therapy

**Clénin G et al.; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.4414/smw.2015.14196). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.4414/smw.2015.14196`. PMID: [26512429](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26512429/).
- **Design / duration:** Consensus / practical review; Monitoring about twice yearly if followed.
- **Population / baseline:** Athletes >15 years; separate child cut-offs.
- **Verified extraction:** Ferritin <15 empty, 15-30 low, 30 µg/L cut-off; 50 µg/L before altitude. Intermittent oral iron if repeatedly low. Long-term daily or IV iron with normal or high ferritin does not make sense and may be harmful.
- **Limitations:** Consensus cut-off, not a universal biological law.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:26512429&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.4414/smw.2015.14196)

<a id="rec62"></a>
### REC62 — Is iron treatment beneficial in iron-deficient but non-anaemic endurance athletes?

**Burden RJ et al.; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1136/bjsports-2014-093624). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1136/bjsports-2014-093624`. PMID: [25361786](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25361786/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis; Varied iron treatments.
- **Population / baseline:** 17 studies; IDNA endurance athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Large effects on ferritin, serum iron, transferrin saturation. Moderate effect on [Hb] and VO2max (Hedges g 0.610, 95% CI 0.399 to 0.821).
- **Limitations:** Deficient athletes only. Not a replete-athlete ergogenic.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)

<a id="rec63"></a>
### REC63 — Effects of Oral Iron Supplementation on Blood Iron Status in Athletes

**Šmid AN et al.; 2024.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-024-01992-8). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s40279-024-01992-8`. PMID: [38407751](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38407751/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review and meta-analysis of RCTs; Oral iron; search to Nov 2022.
- **Population / baseline:** 13 studies, n=449, healthy adult athletes.
- **Verified extraction:** Ferritin SMD 1.27 (0.44 to 2.10), clearest if baseline ≤12 µg/L. Hb and transferrin markers not reliably changed. VO2max SMD 0.49, p=0.086. Evidence moderate to low. Effective trial doses 16-100 mg elemental iron for 6-8 weeks.
- **Limitations:** Not a screening cut-off study.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:38407751&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-024-01992-8)

<a id="rec64"></a>
### REC64 — Iron deficiency, supplementation, and sports performance in female athletes: A systematic review

**Pengelly M et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2024.101009). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1016/j.jshs.2024.101009`. PMID: [39536912](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39536912/).
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review; Varied supplementation.
- **Population / baseline:** 23 studies, 669 high-level female athletes, ferritin <40 µg/L.
- **Verified extraction:** Iron deficiency associated with 3-4% worse endurance. Supplementation improved endurance in included deficient athletes. Most groups ≤20. Quality moderate.
- **Limitations:** Inclusion cut-off <40 is a review definition. Wide improvement ranges.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:39536912&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2024.101009)

- **Limitations:** Arm glove. Iron change not significant.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:18408609&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0b013e3181641c04)

<a id="rec75"></a>
### REC75 — Heat acclimation does not attenuate hepcidin elevation after a single session of endurance exercise under hot condition

**Sumi D et al.; 2022.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-022-04974-8). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-022-04974-8`. PMID: [35674827](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35674827/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled training comparison; 10 days, 8 sessions of 60 min at 50% VO2max in 35°C or 18°C.
- **Population / baseline:** 15 healthy men.
- **Verified extraction:** Hot training lowered core temperature. Hepcidin still rose after a hot exercise test; heat acclimation did not attenuate that rise versus control.
- **Limitations:** Men. Not cyclists specifically. Not a seasonal iron-balance study.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:35674827&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-022-04974-8)

<a id="rec76"></a>
### REC76 — The Prevalence and Impact of Heavy Menstrual Bleeding (Menorrhagia) in Elite and Non-Elite Athletes

**Bruinvels G et al.; 2016.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0149881). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1371/journal.pone.0149881`. PMID: [26901873](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26901873/).
- **Design / duration:** Survey; Cross-sectional.
- **Population / baseline:** 789 online exercisers; 1073 marathon registrants.
- **Verified extraction:** HMB 54% online, 36% marathon, 37% elite respondents. 32% reported anaemia history; 50% had used iron; 22% sought medical advice.
- **Limitations:** Self-report, not measured menstrual iron loss.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Sleep, heat and iron](../topics/sleep-heat-iron.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:26901873&format=json&resultType=core), [source 2](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0149881)

## Strength, warm-ups and mobility

<a id="str01"></a>
### STR01 — Heavy strength training effects on physiological determinants of endurance cyclist performance: a systematic review with meta-analysis

**Llanos-Lagos C, Ramirez-Campillo R, Saez de Villarreal E; 2026.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-025-05883-2). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-025-05883-2`. PMID: [40632222](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40632222/).
**Date note:** First published online 2025-07-09; journal issue 2026. Same review, not two studies.
- **Design / duration:** Systematic review with meta-analysis of controlled trials; Included interventions 5-25 weeks, 1-3 sessions/week; search to February 2025.
- **Population / baseline:** 262 endurance cyclists (60 women); mean baseline VO2max 61.25 ml/kg/min.
- **Verified extraction:** Performance g 0.463 (0.109 to 0.817) is a coherent signed result. Efficiency: small beneficial effect, magnitude about \|g\| 0.353, p=0.012, low certainty. Prose pairs positive point 0.353 with negative 95% CI (-0.619 to -0.088). Figure 3 displays a negative pooled estimate; caption states negative = beneficial. That positive point plus negative interval is a source reporting inconsistency, not a valid signed pair and not a new estimate. Anaerobic power g 0.560; VO2max, pVO2max, MMSS, and anaerobic capacity not significant. GRADE low. Online 2025-07-09; journal issue 2026, 126(1):193-222.
- **Limitations:** Low certainty from risk of bias and imprecision; some non-random allocation; no significant moderators so dose is not optimised. Efficiency text point +0.353 with CI -0.619 to -0.088 conflicts with the negative Figure 3 pool; do not treat that pair as one estimate.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12881108/fullTextXML), [source 2](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/40632222)

<a id="str04"></a>
### STR04 — In-season strength maintenance training increases well-trained cyclists' performance

**Ronnestad BR, Hansen EA, Raastad T; 2010.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-010-1622-4). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1007/s00421-010-1622-4`. PMID: [20799042](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20799042/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled intervention; 12-week build at 2 sessions/week then 13 competition weeks at 1 session/week.
- **Population / baseline:** Well-trained cyclists; E+S n=6 men; E n=6 (5 men, 1 woman).
- **Verified extraction:** Maintenance preserved CSA and 1RM. From the start of the build to mid-competition, Wingate peak, power at 2 mmol/L, Wmax, and 40-min power rose; the last two improved more than endurance-only.
- **Limitations:** Very small.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20799042)

<a id="str05"></a>
### STR05 — Strength training improves performance and pedaling characteristics in elite cyclists

**Ronnestad BR, Hansen J, Hollan I, Ellefsen S; 2015.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12257). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/sms.12257`. PMID: [24862305](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24862305/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled intervention; 25 weeks heavy strength plus endurance.
- **Population / baseline:** Young elite cyclists; ES n=9, E n=7.
- **Verified extraction:** ES improved isometric squat, lower-body lean mass, Wingate peak, Wmax, power at 4 mmol/L, 40-min power, and earlier peak torque. 40-min change correlated with earlier peak torque (r=0.66). VO2max and economy unchanged.
- **Limitations:** Small; not randomised in the retrieved abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/24862305)

<a id="str06"></a>
### STR06 — 10 weeks of heavy strength training improves performance-related measurements in elite cyclists

**Ronnestad BR, Hansen J, Nygaard H; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2016.1215499). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1080/02640414.2016.1215499`. PMID: [27486014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486014/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled intervention; 10 weeks.
- **Population / baseline:** Elite cyclists; E&S n=12, E n=8.
- **Verified extraction:** Larger gains in isometric half-squat and 30-s Wingate mean power. Tendency for power at 4 mmol/L (P=0.068). 40-min all-out 4+/-6% vs -1+/-6%, P=0.13.
- **Limitations:** Short for elites; 40-min difference not significant.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/27486014)

<a id="str07"></a>
### STR07 — Strength training improves cycling performance, fractional utilization of VO2max and cycling economy in female cyclists

**Vikmoen O, Ellefsen S, Troen O, Hollan I, Hanestadhaugen M, Raastad T, Ronnestad BR; 2016.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12468). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/sms.12468`. PMID: [25892654](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25892654/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled intervention; 11 weeks; heavy strength twice weekly.
- **Population / baseline:** 19 well-trained female cyclists; E+S n=11, E n=8.
- **Verified extraction:** 40-min power, fractional utilisation, economy, leg-press 1RM, and quadriceps CSA improved more with strength. IIAX-IIX fell and IIA rose. Performance change correlated with fibre shift (r=-0.63) and CSA (r=0.73).
- **Limitations:** Small; one research group.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/25892654)

<a id="str08"></a>
### STR08 — Heavy strength training improves running and cycling performance following prolonged submaximal work in well-trained female athletes

**Vikmoen O, Ronnestad BR, Ellefsen S, Raastad T; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.14814/phy2.13149). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.14814/phy2.13149`. PMID: [28292885](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28292885/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled intervention; 11 weeks; 3x4-10RM, twice weekly.
- **Population / baseline:** 19 well-trained female duathletes; cycling VO2max 54+/-3 ml/kg/min.
- **Verified extraction:** Half-squat 1RM +45+/-22% and leg lean mass +3.1+/-4.0% more than control. 5-min cycling power after 3 h rose 7.0+/-4.5% in E+S; no change in E. Running 5-min power after 1.5 h also rose, with an explicit between-group difference.
- **Limitations:** Independence from STR07 not established. Between-group p for the cycling 5-min test not stated in the abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/28292885), [source 2](https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC5350167)

<a id="str09"></a>
### STR09 — Maximal strength training improves cycling economy in competitive cyclists

**Sunde A, Storen O, Bjerkaas M, Larsen MH, Hoff J, Helgerud J; 2010.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181aeb16a). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181aeb16a`. PMID: [19855311](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19855311/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled intervention; 8 weeks; half-squat 4x4RM, 3 times/week.
- **Population / baseline:** 13 completers of 16 competitive road cyclists (10 men, 3 women); intervention 7 men and 1 woman.
- **Verified extraction:** 1RM +14.2%, RFD +16.7%, economy at 70% VO2max +4.8%, work efficiency +4.7%, time to exhaustion at pre-intervention maximal aerobic power +17.2%. VO2max and body weight unchanged.
- **Limitations:** Small completer sample; single exercise.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/19855311)

<a id="str10"></a>
### STR10 — Effects of resistance training on endurance capacity and muscle fiber composition in young top-level cyclists

**Aagaard P, Andersen JL, Bennekou M, Larsson B, Olesen JL, Crameri R, Magnusson SP, Kjaer M; 2011.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1600-0838.2010.01283.x). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/j.1600-0838.2010.01283.x`. PMID: [21362056](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21362056/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled concurrent-training comparison; 16 weeks.
- **Population / baseline:** Young elite competitive cyclists, n=14 total; group sizes not in the retrieved abstract.
- **Verified extraction:** MVC and RFD +12-20% with strength. 45-min capacity +8% with strength only. 5-min performance +3-4% in both arms. IIA up and IIX down with strength. VO2max unchanged. Economy improved in endurance-only until it matched the strength group. Capillarisation unchanged.
- **Limitations:** Group sizes not in the retrieved abstract.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/21362056)

<a id="str11"></a>
### STR11 — Influence of conventional resistance training compared to core exercises on road cycling power output

**Sitko S, Lopez-Laval I, Cirer-Sastre R; 2024.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.7759/cureus.59371). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.7759/cureus.59371`. PMID: [38817493](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38817493/).
- **Design / duration:** Three-arm training intervention; 12 weeks; strength or core twice weekly plus matched cycling.
- **Population / baseline:** 36 trained road cyclists, mixed licence levels, 12 per arm.
- **Verified extraction:** Strength superior to core and cycling-only for 5-s, 60-s, 5-min, and 20-min relative power. 20-min adjusted change about 0.22 vs 0.07 and 0.06 W/kg. Core beat cycling-only only at 5 s. Body mass unchanged. Reported VO2max was estimated from 5-min power.
- **Limitations:** Not blinded; mixed performance levels; exploratory.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC11137631/fullTextXML)

<a id="str12"></a>
### STR12 — Off- and on-bike resistance training in cyclists: a randomized controlled trial

**Barranco-Gil D, Hernandez-Belmonte A, Rodriguez-Rielves V, et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39231694/). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1249/MSS.0000000000003556`. PMID: [39231694](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39231694/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled trial; 10 weeks; 2 sessions/week at 70% 1RM; cycling volume and intensity matched.
- **Population / baseline:** Well-trained male cyclists; off-bike n=12, on-bike n=12, control n=13.
- **Verified extraction:** No VO2max effect. Strength rose 2.6-5.8% off-bike and 4.5-7.3% on-bike versus -5.8 to -3.9% in control. Wingate +4.1% and +4.3% versus -4.9%. Quadriceps CSA +2.5% and +2.2% versus -2.3%. Epub 2024-09-03; issue 2025-02-01.
- **Limitations:** Abstract-only. Not a time trial. Treat with STR13 as one programme, not two replications. Load was 70% 1RM, not 4-10RM.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39231694/)

<a id="str13"></a>
### STR13 — Cyclists do not need to incorporate off-bike resistance training to increase strength, muscle-tendon structure, and pedaling performance

**Pallares JG, Barranco-Gil D, Rodriguez-Rielves V, et al.; 2025.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.5114/biolsport.2025.146790). Access: **full-text**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.5114/biolsport.2025.146790`. PMID: [40657002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40657002/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled trial; same design size and protocol as STR12; 10 weeks.
- **Population / baseline:** Well-trained cyclists; VO2max about 62-63 ml/kg/min; weekly cycling about 10.7-10.9 h.
- **Verified extraction:** Both RT arms improved squat force; on-bike uniquely improved pedaling force. MAP rose within RT arms. Time to exhaustion and efficiency had no significant group-by-time interaction. Control lost force and quadriceps size. Off-bike pain and stiffness trends were not significant (ES 0.33 and 0.37).
- **Limitations:** Title overstates substitution. TTE not significant. Same programme as STR12.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC12244400/fullTextXML)

<a id="str14"></a>
### STR14 — Comparison of short-sprint and heavy strength training on cycling performance

**Kristoffersen M, Sandbakk O, Ronnestad BR, Gundersen H; 2019.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.3389/fphys.2019.01132). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.3389/fphys.2019.01132`. PMID: [31555153](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31555153/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised comparison after a shared strength period; 4 weeks shared strength then 6 weeks heavy strength or short sprints.
- **Population / baseline:** 28 competitive cyclists; VO2max 61.1+/-5.9 ml/kg/min.
- **Verified extraction:** Short sprints superior for 6-s, 30-s, and 55 m sprint power. Heavy strength superior for maximal strength. No group difference in endurance-capacity changes.
- **Limitations:** Does not test plyometrics. Gross-efficiency statistic was not cleanly recoverable from the abstract markup.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
- **Retrieval routes:** [source 1](https://europepmc.org/article/MED/31555153)

<a id="str15"></a>
### STR15 — Combining explosive and high-resistance training improves performance in competitive cyclists

**Paton CD, Hopkins WG; 2005.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1519/R-16334.1). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1519/R-16334.1`. PMID: [16287351](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16287351/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled intervention; 4-5 weeks; 12 sessions replacing part of usual training.
- **Population / baseline:** 18 road cyclists; n=9 vs 9.
- **Verified extraction:** Versus control: 1-km power +8.7% (+/-2.5% 90% CL), 4-km +8.1% (+/-4.1%), peak power +6.8%, oxygen cost -3.0%. Sessions mixed single-leg jumps with 5x30-s high-resistance cycling sprints.
- **Limitations:** Not plyometrics alone. Short. Magnitude-based reporting.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)

<a id="str16"></a>
### STR16 — The effects of strength training on endurance performance and muscle characteristics

**Bishop D, Jenkins DG, Mackinnon LT, McEniery M, Carey MF; 1999.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1097/00005768-199906000-00018). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1097/00005768-199906000-00018`. PMID: [10378917](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10378917/).
- **Design / duration:** Randomised controlled intervention; 12 weeks; parallel squat 5 sets to failure at 2-8RM, twice weekly.
- **Population / baseline:** 21 endurance-trained women cyclists, 18-42 years; RT n=14, control n=7.
- **Verified extraction:** Squat 1RM +35.9% versus +3.7%. Authors concluded increased leg strength did not improve cycle endurance performance. Exact 1-h power change was not recoverable from the retrieved abstract.

**Jackson NP, Hickey MS, Reiser RF; 2007.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1519/R-18465.1). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1519/R-18465.1`. PMID: [17313261](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17313261/).

**Beattie K, Carson BP, Lyons M, Kenny IC; 2017.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1123/ijspp.2016-0015). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1123/ijspp.2016-0015`. PMID: [27618339](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27618339/).
- **Design / duration:** Controlled 20-week intervention; 20 weeks maximal and explosive strength.

<a id="str19"></a>
### STR19 — Optimizing strength training for running and cycling endurance performance: a review

**Ronnestad BR, Mujika I; 2014.** [Original source](https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.12104). Access: **abstract**; checked 2026-10-02.

DOI: `10.1111/sms.12104`. PMID: [23914932](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23914932/).
- **Design / duration:** Narrative review; Not a single intervention.
- **Population / baseline:** Endurance-trained runners and cyclists in the cited trials.
- **Verified extraction:** Heavy strength recommended for cycling economy; either heavy or explosive strength can improve running economy. Most compelling additive cycling-performance evidence was for heavy strength.
- **Limitations:** Narrative, superseded in part by the 2025/2026 meta-analysis.
- **Relevant chapters:** [Strength, warm-ups and mobility](../topics/strength-warmups-mobility.md)
